#7355. Correcting the TV ad market’s imperfect shift to streaming

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Evan Rutchik, president and Chief Strategy Officer, Viamedia.ai

The TV ad industry’s shift from linear to streaming is real, but the financial truth underlying that shift may not align with many people’s perception of the transition. That’s because the movement of ad budgets from linear to streaming has hardly been evenly paced.

Streaming’s share of ad revenue is indeed growing at major media companies, including The Walt Disney Company, Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery. But their earnings tell a different story: Linear revenue is falling faster than streaming revenue is rising. The shift is happening, but it’s not benefiting content owners as expected.

The core problem is a supply-demand imbalance. Streaming ad revenue is growing through increased volume, not higher prices. New inventory floods in, making cheap inventory abundant. As major subscription services continue to promote free ad-supported streaming tiers, the buyers’ market will only continue.

The solution is to grow demand, not increase prices. Fortunately, the ad dollars needed to support the streaming market already exist, sitting in local and midmarket budgets that haven’t been able to buy national CTV efficiently. The opportunity is to bring these advertisers into the broader video and streaming market, then develop ways to keep them investing.

Correcting the imbalance requires creating new demand, then ensuring that content quality and product performance are strong enough to eventually shift pricing dynamics.

When it comes to growth, the glut of cheap national SVOD supply may be a problem for programmers, but it’s an opportunity for local and midmarket advertisers. If the current streaming market is largely aimed at Fortune 500 companies, the industry has to consider the thousands of smaller advertisers that make steady annual investments in local linear buys. Taken collectively, these advertisers represent billions of dollars in ad budgets.

Because these advertisers have smaller budgets, many haven’t tried streaming. It is more cost-efficient for them to execute local and spot buys on linear than to invest in targeted or national streaming campaigns. These advertisers are still seeing real success reaching millions of linear viewers, so they understand the power of TV. They just need an entry point for CTV. Solving this issue would allow demand to flow steadily into the market.

Once that demand enters the market, additional steps must happen. CPMs fall when inventory is sold as undifferentiated tonnage. With platforms like Disney+ and Netflix exploring free ad-supported tiers, even more low-cost supply will enter the market. But cheap supply that doesn’t perform isn’t worth an advertiser’s investment. If that inventory doesn’t deliver results for advertisers outside the Fortune 500, those advertisers will invest their budgets elsewhere, creating another demand imbalance.

The answer lies in household addressability. While this is available on some streaming platforms, it’s still largely absent from the overall TV market, including linear. Household-level addressability turns cheap impressions into targeted impressions worth buying, even within highly premium inventory. The more information brands have about who will see their ads and whether those ads are likely to resonate with the viewer, the more they are likely to pay.

It is no secret that streaming is the future of TV, and shifting revenue numbers reflect that. To build a sustainable future for media companies and advertisers, the entire ecosystem must first provide ease of access and then addressability.

Making streaming easier to buy at scale would help solve the demand problem. Once more advertisers are in the market, addressability creates greater competition for impressions, solving the pricing imbalance.

This future is out there, provided the industry takes a few steps in the right direction.

#7352. OpenAI rewrites ChatGPT Business Hub terms after legal language raises concerns

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

OpenAI has revised the terms for its new ChatGPT Business Hub days after its launch. The change came after Digiday reported that lawyers viewed the original language as potentially limiting businesses’ ability to bring certain legal claims against the company.

Launched last week, the Business Hub gives businesses that verify their accounts direct data on how their websites appear in ChatGPT, including mentions, profile views, clicks and discovery topics. For publishers, it’s a first-party view of activity they’ve largely had to estimate.

Before the revision, Section 10 of OpenAI’s terms of service for businesses using ChatGPT appeared to require those businesses to give up certain legal claims against OpenAI, lawyers told Digiday. They said the language could apply to claims involving content published on their websites, before they signed up.

The terms included a mandatory arbitration provision and class-action waiver, potentially requiring businesses to resolve disputes individually and outside of court, Chapell said. The provision was broadly worded to cover certain claims that arose before the agreement was accepted, he explained.

Businesses had 30 days to opt out of arbitration, but if they did not, they could have faced limits on their ability to obtain evidence, pursue appeals and establish legal precedent through public court proceedings, Chapell added.

This could’ve put a chokehold on publishers that are in the midst of figuring out how to protect and monetize their content in the AI era, potentially limiting their ability to bring copyright infringement claims against OpenAI down the road.

Hours after Digiday first contacted OpenAI on Nov. 18, the terms were revised. They now include a waiver of businesses’ rights to seek a court order preventing OpenAI from using certain customer data, according to an IP and tech lawyer, who spoke on the condition of anonymity.

“There are still some restrictions here but they don’t leave a customer completely without recourse. A customer could still, even if not entirely practical, sue for monetary damages,” they said.

When approached for comment, an OpenAI spokesperson said the previous version of the terms “were not intended to cover this scenario,” and confirmed it updated the terms on Nov. 18.

“We continually evaluate the language in our Terms to make sure they are as clear as possible,” the spokesperson added in a statement. “The updated terms replace the separate ‘covered claims’ provision with language in Section 5.2 that ties the release to claims arising from a business’s Business Agent, including the use of customer data with that agent.”

One industry exec, who requested anonymity, told Digiday they advised publishers’ legal teams to be cautious around some of the potential implications of OpenAI’s terms for businesses, after they were originally published on Nov. 10.

Although the terms have been revised, several lawyers who spoke to Digiday said the original version was likely deliberate.

“This is certainly not an oversight because they put these terms out there,” said Gary Kibel, partner at law firm Davis+ Gilbert, which advises media and advertising clients. “It was incredibly intentional, and they were seeking to use this opportunity to get a release and wavier for any of these claims. It seems to be quite aggressive to do that.”

Kibel said the revised terms still require businesses to waive certain legal claims related to their use of ChatGPT Business Hub and its AI Business Agents (essentially a business-specific chatbot), but no longer extend that waiver to unrelated legal disputes with OpenAI.

OpenAI’s changes come just weeks after Meta faced major backlash from publishers over its new commercial terms, which would have allowed the company to scrape the websites of Meta Business account users. The proposed changes spurred a global, coordinated pushback from publishers, trade bodies, and government officials and regulators. In response, Meta announced it would pause the rollout until it has had time to explain the changes to key stakeholders and partners.

OpenAI’s terms revision also follows tensions between publishers and AI companies over the use of published content. Documents unsealed in The New York Times’ lawsuit against OpenAI and Microsoft two months ago revealed tech giants were treating published content as free fuel for AI products that threatened their very existence.

Whatever prompted the revision, it may do little to ease publishers’ broader concerns about how AI companies use their content, and the terms attached to new tools built around that content.

Grand Theft Auto 6 has its own content creator cottage industry, with millions of views and a crowded, competitive marketplace.

ChatGPT’s new AI visibility and click data can help inform publishers’ GEO strategies and complement third-party analytics.

Creators are tapping into their massive content libraries for syndication opportunities that pay dividends.

#7350. Why more brands are paying creators to make their ads

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of an ongoing series detailing the most important information readers need to know about the ad industry’s most complex topics. More from the series →

Brands paying influencers to make campaign creative have become key drivers of investment in the booming creator economy. Creative agencies might want to take note.

“You get the authentic, scroll-stopping content that people want to engage each with. But you also get the reach,” said Katy Ball, head of social at M&C Saatchi Sport & Entertainment.

As spending on paid social channels — and video in particular — continues to rise, demand for creative assets to appear in those ad slots is increasing.

The number of assets attached to individual campaigns has risen 30% in the last five years, according to a CreativeX study of 115,990 ad campaigns, representing $11 billion in ad spend. Per the study, just five assets receive 88% of media investment in an average campaign.

The need for more campaign assets is fueling the growth of asset-led spending, the fastest growing segment of the creator economy, according to a report published this week by research consultancy Madison & Wall.

Currently, 12% of the $112 billion spent on creators globally goes towards creators producing ads on behalf of brands, per the study. That’s expected to rise 16% every year through 2030.

The days when brands principally paid creators to rent their following are rapidly disappearing. Influencers are now media partners, ambassadors and increasingly, cogs in the creative production supply chain. And brands see creator-made ads as a solution to their social asset shortage.

“For us, the appeal is simple. Buying space in someone’s feed doesn’t mean you’ve earned their attention. Good creators understand what their audiences care about and how to give them something worth stopping for,” said Jared Kozel, co-founder and chief creative officer at agency Super Nice. “That’s valuable creative judgment to bring into an ad no matter the brand or category.”

Influencer marketing experts note that several different but related practices are included under the broader umbrella of creator-led production.

While some brands simply pay creators a fee in order to receive video or still assets for use in a campaign, whitelisting (also called “dark advertising”) has become a favored method of managing such arrangements.

In this context, an influencer grants a brand limited access to their social media accounts, allowing them to book a paid media campaign through that account, using assets made by the creator while taking advantage of discrete audience targeting.

“What we’re seeing is a lot of recutting of media spend to be allocated towards creator development in order to fund whitelisting,” said Olivia Wedderburn, head of influence at Born Social.

Madison & Wall’s research suggested that funding for creative production may also be coming from PR, social or experimental budgets, as well as traditional media or production pots of money.

Another related practice, user generated content, sees brands paying regular social media users to license their content as creative assets in ad campaigns.

“They have to feed their their content engines, and they’re opening up as many different avenues as they can,” said Andy Ryan, co-founder at social and influencer agency Komodo.

The relative low cost of working with influencers, versus creative production agencies, is the primary driver behind brand spending on creator-made assets.

Although production spending is often a small fraction of an overall campaign budget, marketers are keen to squeeze every non-working (that is, non-media) dollar for all it’s worth. While that same desire is pushing some brands toward generative AI production techniques, others are turning to creators.

But as this type of spending grows, so to does the need to manage it effectively. “It’s never been more complicated to negotiate usage rights or exclusivity,” said Wedderburn.

Billion Dollar Boy, for example, recently added features to its Companion creator management tool designed to better facilitate creator-led production efforts and manage usage rights negotiations.

“The creator economy has never been more competitive, heightening the urgency for brands to understand the market context,” said Thomas Walters, the agency’s chief innocation officer and co-founder.

At the same time, increased brand spending in this area is already leading to an uptick in the fees commanded by some creators. Last year, agency practitioners reported creator rates ticking up 10-20% due to demand for expanded rights.

There’s also some evidence to show that creator-made assets perform as well as, or even better than, traditional advertising spots in a social context.

A report by System1, WPP Media and Goat published in June found that creator ads achieved 3.2X the brand-awareness lift for category leader ads as traditional advertising creative.

“We’re seeing a lot of brands say that is more valuable in terms of conversion and reach than some of the quick-and-dirty assets they previously would have relied on,” said Wedderburn.

That’s not unexpected. Audiences recognize the visual cues, language and production methods employed by creators as native to TikTok or Instagram, while clocking films shot on a closed set or using more traditional methods as foreign that same environment, a lesson that major advertisers like Nike are reluctantly absorbing.

“If programs are set up correctly, creators can deliver volume and variety faster and more cost effectively than a traditional production cycle,” said Nicholas Spiro, chief commercial officer, Viral Nation. “The shift we’re seeing is brands starting to plan creators across their media budgets, rather than as a separate influencer line item alone.”

Consulting giants Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

With shareholders, an $895 billion compute bill and a costly free tier, Anthropic’s no-ads pledge may have a shelf life.

Why the Daily Mail’s ad sales team is turning talent agent.

#7346. Anthropic says no to ads but Wall Street may have other ideas

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Anthropic is a public company now, which puts it on an unofficial path to an ads business.

Unofficial because the company’s official position is still firmly against ads. It went as far as spending Super Bowl money on a campaign mocking OpenAI for bringing them to ChatGPT. Its prospectus, the document pitching those public shares to investors, leaves advertisers out of the business altogether.

“Is it cynical to expect Anthropic to follow the same route? Not really. More like an educated guess,” said ad tech expert Shirley Marschall.

Besides, the company has already left the door open, she continued. Its pledge rules out sponsored links adjacent to conversations, but in the same post it says it will keep building features that help users “find, compare, or buy products, connect with businesses.” And once an agent recommends products, retail media will pay to be in those recommendations, Marschall said, before adding: “Let’s see Anthropic turn that ad revenue down,” she added.

Advertisers, for their part, will be watching closely, and with mixed feelings. On the one hand, a new media seller means a new platform to wrangle and one more set of tools and teams to learn at a time when most are still working out how to buy ChatGPT. On the other hand, Claude’s audience is also spending more and more time in those conversation threads. For marketers, that’s a hard place to stay out of for long.

“Anthropic, like any company, has a stance towards handling consumer data and inadvertently how advertising is considered,” said independent ad tech consultant Jonathan D’Souza Rauto. “But monetization is a lucrative form of revenue. I’d expect Anthropic to have a change of heart. Look no further to how Apple has played this advertising game over the past few years despite being considered anti-advertising.”

Life as a public company isn’t cheap, after all, and it’s even less cheap for an AI company that has signed up for approximately $895.4 billion in compute and infrastructure commitments, some running to 2045. That’s more than 13 years of revenue at its current pace, given Q3 revenue of $17 billion. The revenue covering that bill is far less locked in since 80% of revenue is consumption-based and the S-1 says many customers can cut usage or terminate on short notice. In fact, the company has already warned that “operating margins may compress when large tranches arrive.”

Then there’s the free tier. “Free and promotional users may never convert to paid plans,” the filing admits, yet Anthropic still pays to serve every one of them. Analysts found that cost sits in sales and marketing out of sight of the headline margin. That arrangement holds as long as free users keep upgrading. If they stop, Anthropic is left with a growing audience it pays to serve and earns nothing from.

OpenAI has already shown what that audience could be worth. ChatGPT ads reached a $1 billion annualized revenue run rate less than 200 days after launching. When both companies are trading, analysts will ask Anthropic why it’s leaving money on the table.

“They’ll go after advertising, just not in the way some assume,” said Rob Webster, founder and CEO of TAU Marketing Solutions. “The prize for Anthropic isn’t ads inside Claude. Advertising is now a trillion-dollar market, and the real power sits with whatever brain coordinates it: deciding what runs, where, and why. That’s a far more valuable position than selling impressions. Anthropic wants to be a new, better Microsoft or Apple. Selling tools to businesses. Not ad opportunities.”

There are reasons, though, why Anthropic may resist the lure of ads for as long as it feasibly can.

“Trust and impartiality really matter with LLMs, particularly among enterprise customers,” said Alex Tait, founder of Entropy. “In contrast to OpenAI, Anthropic is currently approaching operating break-even without ads suggesting its existing business model could be commercially sustainable without it.”

Anthropic’s anti-ads stance is based on its perspective that once ads become part of a business’s revenue targets, the pressure to maximize engagement can start influencing product decisions. For an AI app, in this case Claude, that creates a conflict: users expect Claude to help them make decisions in their own interests, not towards whatever generates revenue for the company or its advertisers. And in its IPO prospectus, the company said that they want “Claude’s only incentive to be giving them [users] a helpful answer,” which is why they have “chosen not to introduce that pressure [ads] into Claude.”

Anthropic did not respond to Digiday’s request for comment.

Consulting giants Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

#7343. Future of Marketing Briefing: Consulting firms revive their marketing ambitions in the C-suite the holdcos covet

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of Marketing Briefing covers the latest in marketing for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Friday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

However the holdcos settle the question of whether the CEO route counts as due diligence, Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song got there first, and both have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

Deloitte Digital’s offer is Converge, an operating system that strings together AI agents to run marketing and sales work, from research and briefs through to campaign deployment and deal tracking. Some of the agents are Deloitte’s own. Others are ones a client already runs elsewhere, and the whole thing plugs into the tools and clout contracts a brand is already paying for. Marketing and sales versions are live, with commerce on the way, and Deloitte pitches them as one front-office system tied to revenue, which it calls “brand to cash.” Because it’s sold as a license that stretches across sales, marketing and service, it needs sign-off well above the CMO, which suits a consulting firm that’s used to having those conversations.

Accenture Song’s play in media, a market it has circled in the U.S. for years without committing. In September, it moved Melissa Fein, who built its media planning and buying business in Australia and New Zealand after leaving Initiative in 2024, to New York as U.S. media lead. This month it posted a job for a head of media strategy for the Americas. The ad describes a “new world media practice” with no legacy systems to protect, and asks for an AI-first strategist” to lead planning, build new tools and become the person senior clients call before a brief exists.

Candidates need hands-on command of econometrics, incrementality testing and attribution plus experience in big-spending categories like financial services, telecommunications, automotive and health. Those are the categories where the ANZ team won Australia Post and Mediabank. Accenture Song Ndidi Oteh has kept quiet on specifics, saying only that whatever it does in media won’t take traditional forms.

“The real prize is the conversation before the brief,” said  Steve Boehler, founder of agency consultancy the Mercer Island Group. ”If you help define the business problem, design the operating model and decide how success will be measured, you are competing long before an agency review begins.”

Both plays echo what the holdcos have spent 18 months engineering: operating systems that run a client’s marketing for it and media businesses that sell more than buying. The consultants may have the better shot at making either one stick. They already sit with the CEOs, CIOs and CFOs who sign off on systems spanning sales, marketing and service and installing that kind of software is their day job. A holdco operating system, rightly or wrongly, still reads as an agency tool, built partly to make the agency cheaper to run. Accenture also arrives in the U.S. media with no legacy buying business to protect, so it can lead with measurement and tools before it has to sell buying power.

Whether any of this translates is another matter. Consultancies have been trying to crack advertising for the best part of a decade, after all, and the media buying business still rewards a scale Accenture doesn’t yet have in the U.S. Then again, now is arguably as good a time as any for them to try, if not better. With Omnicom and IPG combined, big advertisers are down to three holdcos, and some are skipping the pitch one so a credible alternative has rarely been more useful. AI is also pushing decisions about how marketing works into rooms where the CFO and CIO sit, which consultancies have been in for years. And a C-suite that takes a closer look at marketing will start asking how agencies make their money, from data fees to principal media, which is an awkward question for any firm with a buying business to defend.

“The consultancies may have an advantage in getting invited to design the system,” said Boehler. “That is not the same as proving they can run marketing better. The client still needs evidence of better decisions, better work and better business outcomes, not just fewer handoffs.”

All of which makes the Publicis-WPP episode look a little quaint, partly because it was never quite the fight it was billed as. Sadoun spent months telling investors that Publicis was winning big accounts without a pitch. Rose, asked about it by an analyst, called it “this pitchless CEO handshake thing that I keep getting asked about” and said decisions that big deserve careful consideration. Publicis answered with an open letter treating that line as her theory, and the coverage did the rest. Somewhere along the way, a question about how clients choose agencies turned into a row about manners.

What got lost was the shift both CEOs were describing. Big advertisers are increasingly buying data, identity and AI infrastructure, which a six-week parade of slide decks was never built to judge, so some now test one partner in depth instead of auditioning several. Those decisions are also moving up the organization chart, to executives who sign off on systems not campaigns. That’s how consultancies have always been bought. Deloitte and Accenture are built for long, technical evaluations run by CFOs and CIOs, and while the holdcos; remarks were being turned into a grudge march one launched a system that needs the c-suite’s signature and the other started building a U.S. media practice designed to win it.

“Client CEOs and CMOs pay attention to CEOs of holdcos, especially when a transition in agency leadership occurs and the agency CEO conducts outreach to key clients,” said Gartner analyst Jay Wilson. “But most recognize that while agency CEOs may set a direction for the agency, that direction may be slow to take hold – or never take hold – within the agency teams doing the actual day-to- day work.  A strong relationship with, or aversion to, an agency chief executive is usually more consequential at smaller, independent agencies.”

Whatever gets agreed in the c-suite, someone still has to do the work.

“The consultants don’t have to copy the incumbent model to compete,” said Boehler. “But they still have to do the work: plan, negotiate, deliver, reconcile and prove results. A different model is a proposition until clients experience the difference.”

Cinema admissions have climbed back on the strength of a fuller blockbuster slate and much of the ad money that shifted to digital during lockdowns stayed put. Most screens still sell their preshow through sales houses that have always sold offline.

Mikkel Hagedorn, a WPP and Omnicom alum, has spent five or six years building a platform that lets buyers book cinema spots programmatically, target them against specific films and count impressions straight off ticket sales. The company is live in Denmark and Finland, where sales houses have handed it two minutes of a 15-minute preshow at a base CPM of around €75. Hagedorn’s bet is that exhibitors can bring cinema into the digital ad market while keeping the premium pricing newspapers gave away when they went programmatic.

His company, called Cinemataztic, gives buyers two ways to purchase inventory: first, agencies already running programmatic campaigns can reach the inventory through supply-side platforms and demand-side platforms such as Smartclip Google’s DV360 respectively, which lets cinema sit inside budgets that were going to be spread across other channels anyway, Advertisers who would rather skip the intermediary fees can book directly through Hagedorn’s own interface.

Either way, the buyer can pick what individual film they want to target, so a brand could run only against the upcoming Avengers: Doomsday movie if it likes. The impressions they buy come straight from exhibitors’ ticketing systems, which means one ticket sold counts as one impression. There are “no multipliers here,” said Hagedorn, a dig at the audience multipliers that inflate numbers elsewhere in media.

There’s also a performance bent to the inventory. Audiences log into a pre-show game, which feeds real-time targeting data and lets advertisers push vouchers to their phones. Hagedorn pointed to a Specsavers campaign as an example. On lead generation campaigns, he claimed an average click-through rate of 10%, a figure display buyers stopped dreaming about years ago.

So far a lot of the money has come from digital agencies edging up the funnel into brand work. Hagedorn said he has seen little cannibalisation of advertisers still buying cinema the old way. He said the business has grown 50% a year and forecasts the same for next year, when the company expands into Sweden, Thailand and a third, bigger market he wouldn’t share.

Even so, the programmatication of cinema will be a slow burn. Advertising is still its third-largest revenue line after tickets and concessions. Most hand it entirely to sales houses on contracts of three to five years and only learn that demand is falling when the next guarantee shrinks. Hagedorn has been warning them about this for years. “They cannot stay offline forever,” he said.

Brands that gatecrash cultural moments tend to stick to tournaments and royal weddings, and they rarely pick a game that lets players mug strangers in a fake Florida. Grand Theft Auto, the culture-warping franchise, is the exception, and its sixth installment is drawing the kind of guerilla marketing normally reserved for FIFA and the Olympics.

Anyone trying to keep up can follow the running tally at GTA6Brands.com, an independent tracker run by Mike Litman that logs a new stunt each day for his GTA 6 Marketing News podcast. Hardly anything in culture still sets off that kind of scramble across so many brands at once, and with GTA the explanation is scale.

“I’ve tracked over 290 brand moves around GTA 6 so far, including around 130 unofficial activations,” said Litman. “That’s a remarkable amount of marketing activity orbiting a product launch, particularly when so many of those brands have no formal relationship with Rockstar. What’s interesting is the range of brands getting involved.”

With media this fragmented, marketers have a shortlist of moments when a mass audience shows up at the same time, and most of those moments involve a ball. GTA earned its spot on that list over 13 years. When GTA 5 launched in 2013 it made $1 billion in three days, which owner Take Two called a rate faster than any other video game, film or other entertainment product has ever managed. For context, Marvel’s “The Avengers” movie took 19 days to reach that number. In the years since it launched, the game has since surpassed 230 million units sold worldwide across three console generations. The wait for the sequel turned into an event of its own. GTA 6’s second trailer received 475 million views across all platforms within 24 hours of its release, beating the 365 million in 24 hours for Marvel’s Deadpool & Wolverine film. Take Two has issued $8 billion for its 2027 guidance for the fiscal year the game launches in. For a brand that wants a crowd all watching the same thing at once, that’s about as close to a World Cup final as entertainment gets, and nobody has to get past FIFA’s lawyers.

“I think the fascinating thing is that GTA 6 has achieved something usually reserved for the World Cup or Super Bowl, but without the stadium, the broadcast rights or the traditional sponsorship machinery,” said Litman. “It’s become a cultural event in its own right. And brands aren’t waiting for permission to participate.”

A few did get permission, though. Rockstar’s list of official partners is short and made up mostly of platforms. Sony signed an exclusive marketing deal, which put a “Plays Best on PS5” tag on the game’s official channels. Netflix got the “Extended Look” trailer six trailers before YouTube in August, in what Take Two boss Strauss Zelnick called a “groundbreaking partnership”. YouTube is running a 24-hour launch live livestream from its own channel, and the Miami Heat have redesigned court, custom uniforms and Vice City merchandise.

“Nike is going back to its roots and what built the brand: cultural relevance through people who have the trust of a community. It is a bold choice when most advertising pitches are about AI. Nike uses AI too, as a tool, but clearly shows it understands that what stays scarce is trust. Trust comes from identity and proximity: who is speaking, and how close they are to the community listening. It also shows what creators and athletes must build to ask a brand like Nike for more than just a campaign fee. Most of them are offering access to an audience that belongs to a social or tech platform, targeted and measured with the data that the platform makes available. Global stars aside, only those who directly own the relationship with their audience and their data can negotiate a share of the long-term value.”

— Alessandro De Zanche, founder of media consultancy ADZ Strategies on Nike’s bet on creators to drive growth.

#7334. Growth Copywriter at Winona

Winona is one of the leading telemedicine companies providing HRT for women in menopause. We’ve built all of our technology in house including an EHR, patient care admin system, patient portal, website, marketing technology etc. Winona has two compounding pharmacies and a team of in house physicians providing world-class care via a fully vertically integrated business model.

We are looking for a data-backed, empathetic Growth Copywriter to own the performance and optimization of our end-to-end messaging ecosystem. At Winona, copy isn’t just about sounding nice. It’s a measurable variable in our growth funnel. You will design and execute rapid copy experiments across our paid acquisition, intake flows, and lifecycle retention loops to help women confidently access bioidentical HRT and menopause care.

Full-Funnel Experimentation. Write copy hypotheses, design rapid A/B tests alongside growth and analytics teams, analyze performance data, and turn winning variations into a scalable messaging playbook.

Lifecycle Growth & Retention. Write, test, and optimize automated onboarding, activation, adherence, churn-save, and win-back sequences across email, SMS, and push notifications to drive LTV.

High-Intent Conversion Surfaces. Own the persuasive strategy and microcopy for landing pages, interactive quiz/intake flows, checkouts, and dynamic pricing/offer messaging.

Paid Acquisition at Scale. Generate volume-driven concepts, hooks, and static/video copy variants for Meta, Google, and YouTube, systematically iterating based on creative testing data.

Voice of Customer (VoC) Intelligence. Continuously mine product reviews, customer support tickets, intake data, and exit surveys to isolate deep qualitative insights and high-converting customer language.

AI-Augmented Workflows. Leverage and train Winona’s internal AI copywriting tools to scale production velocity, maintain brand consistency, and build automated variant pipelines without sacrificing quality.

5+ years of dedicated copywriting experience specializing in direct-response, conversion copywriting, or growth marketing.

DTC Telehealth, Wellness, or HealthTech experience is highly preferred. You understand how to write about sensitive health topics with clinical authority and deep empathy.

A portfolio backed by data and results — the exact copy variants you tested, the structural or psychological changes you made, and the quantifiable lift they produced in metrics like CTR, quiz completion, or conversion rates.

Deep lifecycle marketing mastery: significant experience building and optimizing automated email and SMS flows (onboarding, retention, win-backs), rather than just writing one-off promotional campaigns. You know how to nurture a lead over time.

A strong foundation in A/B testing mechanics — comfortable formulating clear copy hypotheses, understanding sample size requirements, and knowing when a test result has achieved true statistical significance.

The balance of empathy and compliance: a proven ability to write about highly sensitive personal health topics (menopause symptoms, hormonal changes, wellness) with profound empathy while strictly adhering to healthcare compliance rules and ad-network medical restrictions.

An experimenter’s ego — you don’t take copy critiques personally. You’re obsessed with letting consumer behavior and performance data crown the winning angle.

As a full-time member of our team, you’ll enjoy:

Paid non-working holidays per country of residence

Medical/ Dental/ Vision insurance (US-W2)

Employee engagement activities and virtual gatherings

Winona provides equal employment opportunities to all qualified individuals without regard to race, color, religion, sex, gender identity, sexual orientation, pregnancy, age, national origin, physical or mental disability, military or veteran status, genetic information, or any other protected classification. Equal employment opportunity extends to all aspects of employment including, but not limited to, recruitment, hiring, training, promotion, demotion, transfer, leaves of absence, and termination. Winona is one of the leading telemedicine companies providing HRT for women in menopause. It’s a measurable variable in our growth funnel. You will design and execute rapid copy experiments across our paid acquisition, intake flows, and lifecycle retention loops to help women confidently access bioidentical HRT and menopause care.

Lifecycle Growth & Retention. Write, test, and optimize automated onboarding, activation, adherence, churn-save, and win-back sequences across email, SMS, and push notifications to drive LTV.

AI-Augmented Workflows. Leverage and train Winona’s internal AI copywriting tools to scale production velocity, maintain brand consistency, and build automated variant pipelines without sacrificing quality.

5+ years of dedicated copywriting experience specializing in direct-response, conversion copywriting, or growth marketing.

DTC Telehealth, Wellness, or HealthTech experience is highly preferred. You understand how to write about sensitive health topics with clinical authority and deep empathy.

A portfolio backed by data and results — the exact copy variants you tested, the structural or psychological changes you made, and the quantifiable lift they produced in metrics like CTR, quiz completion, or conversion rates.

Deep lifecycle marketing mastery: significant experience building and optimizing automated email and SMS flows (onboarding, retention, win-backs), rather than just writing one-off promotional campaigns. You know how to nurture a lead over time.

The balance of empathy and compliance: a proven ability to write about highly sensitive personal health topics (menopause symptoms, hormonal changes, wellness) with profound empathy while strictly adhering to healthcare compliance rules and ad-network medical restrictions.

An experimenter’s ego — you don’t take copy critiques personally. You’re obsessed with letting consumer behavior and performance data crown the winning angle.

#7321. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer at Clutch

Clutch builds AI agents that work on behalf of credit unions. Emma, our collections agent, is live at five credit unions and handled 60,000+ member conversations in her first twelve weeks at one of them. Behind her is a growing portfolio: origination, fraud step-up, member (re)engagement and activation agents, all built on the same Agents Platform (TypeScript, NestJS, our own agent SDK, MCP, Temporal, promptfoo).

As a FDE you are one of the two engineers on an agent. You take it from "sold" to "live and performing" at a specific credit union: you sit in the discovery sessions, you build the integrations to their core banking and loan systems, you author and tune the agent's behavior, you run the live tests with their staff, and you stay with it through go-live and the first weeks of production. When something needs a person in the room, you are the one who flies.

This is a senior engineering role, not a solutions or support role. You write production code in our monorepo every week: integrations, modules, and the agents themselves (prompts, tools, evals), together with the other engineers on the pod. The difference from a traditional engineer is that you are on site more often, you make judgment calls in front of the customer, and you own the outcome. What you learn in the field flows straight back into the product: defaults, playbooks, and the platform capabilities the next agent will need.

You build; our AI Operations Managers deliver. The pod (pod lead plus engineers) designs and builds each agent in a high-touch way with the first customers. Once the agent is defined and live, AI Ops coordinates the implementations, configuration and ongoing success at the next credit unions. You hand them something that works and a playbook to run it.

This role requires roughly 20% of travel on average, with one- to two-week stretches around kickoffs and go-lives. Based in the US, able to travel on short notice

You'll join the Agents team, a small, senior group operating as a startup inside Clutch: seven engineers, a dedicated AI product group, and AI sales specialists, working directly with some of the largest credit unions in the country. Every agent is staffed by a pair of engineers, one leaning toward the conversation and LLM side, one toward software and integrations, with a pod lead who owns the product day to day. There is no layer between you and the customer, and no layer between you and the platform team.

The operating style is direct, written, and evidence-driven: BLUF updates, evals over opinions, business outcomes over ticket counts. The team is spread across Brazil, Argentina and the US and works async by default. Being on site is a tool we use at specific moments (kickoff, live testing, go-live), not a permanent posting.

Ship your first production change to a live agent through the team's review process, and be able to explain how a member's call flows through the Agents Platform end to end

Learn one agent deeply: system prompt, tool contracts, orchestration triggers, compliance gates, and the implementation process from kickoff to go-live

Join at least one credit union implementation as the second engineer, including a live testing session with their staff

Map where the current implementation process loses time and where a default or a tool would have removed a round trip

Own the technical side of one credit union implementation end to end: core and LOS integration, agent configuration, testing, go-live, and the first weeks of production

Run the eval and tuning loop for your agent (golden transcripts, rubric judges, regression suite) and be trusted to change its behavior without a second engineer checking your work

Have completed at least two on-site visits and turned what you heard there into product decisions with your pod lead and PM

Contribute at least one reusable capability back to the Agents Platform that the next implementation will not have to rebuild

Have taken one net-new agent from sold to live at its first credit union, with data you can show the partner

Be the engineer credit union staff ask for by name when something in their deployment needs to change

Have written the implementation playbook for your agent well enough that the AI Ops team runs the second and third credit union without you

Be shaping what the next agents look like, because you have seen where the current ones break in the field

7+ years of software engineering in a fast-paced environment, preferably in SaaS and/or Fintech, with senior TypeScript and Node.js backend experience in a large codebase (NestJS, CQRS, schema-validated boundaries, dependency injection). We do not count years of AI experience; the field is too young for that to mean anything. We look at what you have shipped and how you reason about it

Integration and API engineering: secure service-to-service endpoints, third-party APIs, webhooks, and the patience to work with legacy banking systems that were not designed to be integrated

Security and auth judgment: OAuth and machine-to-machine auth, tenant-scoped access, secrets handling, no PII in logs. Our agents touch sensitive member data and operate under consumer-contact regulation

Customer presence: you can sit across from a VP of Collections, hear a request, and say "here is what I recommend and why", including when the answer is no. You are comfortable making calls in the room and owning them afterwards

Code-quality judgment under deadline pressure: you take on debt deliberately, name it, and pay it down, rather than gold-plating or leaving silent debt behind

Genuine appetite for LLM agents: eval discipline for non-deterministic systems, reading a system prompt like code, understanding that some decisions must never reach the model. Curiosity plus shipped work is enough; research depth is not required

Nice to have, explicitly not required: voice and telephony (LiveKit, Twilio), Temporal or event-driven orchestration, Python, core banking or LOS integration experience, credit union or banking domain knowledge

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

Unforgettable Off-Sites: Twice a year, bond with colleagues in exciting destinations, fostering teamwork and fresh ideas.

Paid Time Off: Enjoy flexible PTO days yearly for relaxation and rejuvenation.

Stock Options: Joining us means having a stake in our success, so you'll receive stock options as part of your compensation package.

Home Office Setup: Create your ideal workspace with a dedicated budget for home office essentials.

Work Trip Budget: Grow personally and professionally with a budget for work-related trips and co-working.

Health Coverage: Prioritize your well-being with comprehensive health and dental insurance plans.

Clutch is a revolutionary vertical SaaS company, proudly backed by Andreessen Horowitz (A16z), aimed at revolutionizing the way Credit Unions engage and change the lives of their members. As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt. Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. Our mission extends beyond mere financial transactions; we strive to fundamentally enhance the way credit unions interact with their members. By integrating cutting-edge technologies and user-centric designs, we help credit unions provide seamless digital experiences that are on par with leading tech companies. This approach not only preserves but revitalizes the longstanding tradition of community and member-focused service inherent to credit unions.

The Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit is the technical owner and architect of the systems that power our go-to-market engine. You'll decide how our GTM stack is designed, what we build versus buy, and where we invest next — and you'll be hands-on building it: Salesforce architecture, integrations, enrichment pipelines, and the agentic workflows that help Sales and Marketing move faster and smarter.

Salesforce is our system of record for revenue, pipeline, and customer data. HubSpot is our marketing layer for campaigns and lead generation, and what it captures needs to flow cleanly and reliably into Salesforce. Around them sits a growing stack of enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and data platform tools. You'll own the architecture that ties it all together: disciplined change management, observable pipelines, data models built for AI consumption, and agentic workflows that act on GTM signals at scale — designed to serve the business for years, not quarters.

This role sits at the intersection of GTM, Data, Finance, and Engineering. You'll work directly with senior leaders to shape the GTM systems roadmap, turn business strategy into technical plans, and guide decisions that span multiple teams. You'll also help grow the engineers around you, setting the technical standards and practices the team builds on.

You understand the difference between owning a system and gatekeeping one. Sales lives in Salesforce. Marketing lives in HubSpot. Your job is to make sure both are governed well enough that others can work in them effectively, and powerful enough that the GTM team can do things they couldn't do before.

You'll thrive as a Staff GTM Systems Engineer if you:

Are known as the go-to person for tough technical problems, and have good judgment about which ones matter most.

Think in systems and long time horizons, and can make confident architectural decisions with incomplete information.

Can clearly explain complex technical concepts and tradeoffs to engineers and executives alike.

Believe systems should be managed with engineering discipline.

Ship fast and reliably — your work holds up in production.

Are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools.

Find real satisfaction in mentoring other engineers and helping them grow.

Own the architecture of the GTM systems stack: define how Salesforce, HubSpot, enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and the data platform fit together, and write the technical strategy that guides how it evolves.

Lead build-vs-buy and procurement decisions: evaluate vendors, run proofs of concept, assess security and integration fit, and partner with Director of GTM Systems and Analytics, Finance, Legal, and IT on selection and renewals.

Shape the GTM systems roadmap with senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, RevOps, Finance, and Engineering: align on requirements, sequence investments, make clear tradeoffs, and lead cross-functional initiatives to completion.

Own Salesforce as the engineering system of record for revenue: data model, flows and automation, validation rules, field governance, and access control.

Own HubSpot for campaigns and lead generation, along with the HubSpot-Salesforce sync — so leads, contacts, and engagement signals reach the revenue pipeline without drift or silent failures.

Build and maintain integrations across the GTM stack, and partner with GTM Analytics Engineering on reliable data flows to the central data platform.

Design and deploy agentic workflows that consume GTM data, surface actionable signals, and automate manual operations — and set the patterns and guardrails others build on.

Establish observability and change management for critical GTM systems: alerts, dashboards, and runbooks; reviewed code changes, documented config, and rollback paths.

Mentor engineers across experience levels and set the technical standards for the team.

Partner with the Billing Systems Engineer to automate the CPQ-to-Billing handoff.

Extensive engineering experience in GTM or business systems, including owning a GTM stack's architecture end to end — build-vs-buy calls, vendor evaluations, and systems designed for a multi-year horizon.

Deep, hands-on Salesforce expertise — you understand the data model, have built complex flows and automation, and you think about it as an engineering system, not an admin tool. This is the primary bar.

Technical depth beyond the UI — you've built integrations with APIs, webhooks, and event streams, and you know when a native tool (a Flow, a Clay table, a HubSpot workflow) is the right answer versus when it's time to write code.

#7315. Senior Manager, Business Operations at Clutch

Clutch is at a pivotal moment. We're growing significantly year over year, and we need more connective tissue to keep pace. We're hiring a Senior Manager, Business Operations to connect the commercial side of the house, so the teams that win, onboard, and retain our customers work from the same facts and toward the same goals. This role sits in Finance.

This is a people manager role. You'll lead our Revenue Operations (RevOps) team and act as an operating partner to senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, Customer Success, Implementation, People, and Finance. Together with the RevOps team, you own the shared language of the business (definitions, data, and processes) so commercial teams make decisions from one view of it.

When something important doesn't have an owner yet, it lands with you: a partnership strategy, a new commercial model, or pricing and margin guidance for leadership. This is a fast-moving startup. We're building toward a much larger company but we aren't one yet, and most of this role has no playbook. You'll define the process, build the tool, and hand it to the team. If you want established structure, this likely isn't the role.

You'll report to the Director of Business Operations and work directly with senior leaders across the company as a neutral partner to every function rather than an advocate for one. Your work will be visible, and it will significantly influence how we make commercial decisions.

You'll lead a small, high-ownership RevOps team that runs the systems and data our commercial teams depend on, and creates leverage for them to do their jobs even better. The team doesn't just own systems. They build automation, tooling, and processes, and they're close partners to Sales and Marketing. You'll coach them, set priorities, and open up more strategic scope, while giving them real autonomy.

Learn the business end to end: the revenue funnel, the customer lifecycle, how each commercial team works day to day, and where decisions get stuck

Build trust with your RevOps team: learn the work they own, and set priorities and team rhythms together

Deliver the first version of client-level margin and cost-to-serve, the analysis that grounds our pricing strategy

Develop a first point of view on pricing and packaging for revenue leadership, built on that analysis

Put AI to work in your own workflow from week one, and identify the first set of manual, repetitive processes across the commercial teams worth automating

Stand up a first version of a deal desk, including an approval workflow, pricing and discount guardrails, and standard commercial terms

Partner with Sales and Marketing leaders as they prepare for the monthly business review: pressure-test their plans and point out risks, then solve them with the RevOps team by creating new processes, tooling, or automation

Build on the strength of the RevOps team: prioritize its roadmap against business outcomes and take on more strategic work together

Launch your first AI-enabled workflows or automations that remove manual reporting or reconciliation for the commercial teams, with time saved tracked

Have a team whose output and standards hold, with the processes, tooling, and automation you've built running without you in the room

Have AI-enabled, self-serve tooling that commercial teams use day to day, with measurable time savings

Be the person leadership trusts to pick up the next important thing that doesn't have an owner yet

5-8 years of total experience that combines consulting or banking with time as an operator inside a company

Experience managing people: you've coached ICs, given direct feedback, made tough talent calls, and built team rhythms

Startup experience. You've worked in a fast-moving, ever-changing environment where structure wasn't already there, and you built it. This is not a role for someone whose experience is only at large, established enterprises.

Strong analytical and financial modeling skills, including forecasting. You're very comfortable building models in spreadsheets from scratch, and they hold up to scrutiny. You use AI to move faster and sharpen your work, and you have the foundational modeling knowledge to know when its output is wrong.

Data-first and self-sufficient. You open the dataset before you build the slide, you're always looking for more data to test an idea, and you're comfortable getting into raw data yourself. You use AI to analyze it faster and know enough to check its work.

Commercial acumen: you can read commercial terms, spot the problematic ones, and reason about pricing, discounting, margin, and deal structure

A clear communicator who can walk an executive through a messy problem and leave them with a decision, and who is just as effective working alongside an AE, a CSM, or an engineer

Comfort with ambiguity. When something isn't set up yet, your instinct is to build it, not to wait for it. You create structure, manage competing priorities, and pivot when things change.

Relentless follow-through: you close loops, and people stop checking on what you've said you'll do

A partner's temperament: you challenge constructively without taking sides, you surface problems early, and you resolve cross-team conflict without escalating it

Industry experience in fintech, financial services, lending, banking, or credit unions

Please note that this role may evolve as our business needs change, so we appreciate your flexibility and adaptability.

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

Unforgettable Off-Sites: Twice a year, bond with colleagues in exciting destinations, fostering teamwork and fresh ideas.

Paid Time Off: Enjoy flexible PTO days yearly for relaxation and rejuvenation.

Stock Options: Joining us means having a stake in our success, so you'll receive stock options as part of your compensation package.

Home Office Setup: Create your ideal workspace with a dedicated budget for home office essentials.

Work Trip Budget: Grow personally and professionally with a budget for work-related trips and co-working.

Health Coverage: Prioritize your well-being with comprehensive health and dental insurance plans.

Clutch is a revolutionary vertical SaaS company, proudly backed by Andreessen Horowitz (A16z), aimed at revolutionizing the way Credit Unions engage and change the lives of their members. As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt.  Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. Our mission extends beyond mere financial transactions; we strive to fundamentally enhance the way credit unions interact with their members. By integrating cutting-edge technologies and user-centric designs, we help credit unions provide seamless digital experiences that are on par with leading tech companies. This approach not only preserves but revitalizes the longstanding tradition of community and member-focused service inherent to credit unions.

Clutch is an equal opportunity employer. All applicants will be considered for employment without attention to race, color, religion, sex, sexual orientation, gender identity, national origin, veteran or disability status.

Please approach interviews without the use of AI tools—our goal is to get to know how you think, solve problems, and communicate. Once you're in the seat, bring on the prompts!

#7294. Skydance’s Rebecca Mall Announces New Marketing Leadership Team

Skydance’s marketing division is the latest unit to reveal its new leadership structure. Following its acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery in October, Skydance has been busy working to mesh the operations of the two media companies.

Chief marketing officer Rebecca Mall, in a memo to her team, announced the new vision for the marketing team. “We have an incredible opportunity to build a more unified, innovative and effective marketing organization – one that breaks down silos, understands our audiences better than anyone and harnesses the power of creativity, data and technology to set a new standard for our industry,” according to the memo.

She added that the team’s job is not just to market what they create, but also to help drive growth and success for the entire Skydance company.

Mall revealed that cross-company marketing and corporate brand, data & analytics, media, partnerships, and franchise and kids & family will now be housed under one roof. The new leadership team includes Annah Zafrani, who will be tasked with leading Skydance’s cross-company marketing and brand unit. Zafrani, who comes from Paramount Skydance, will coordinate priority campaigns across the media company’s businesses and lead corporate brand marketing. Reporting to her will be, also from the Paramount Skydance side, Katie Hutchings, svp, brand marketing, creative and strategy, and her team.

Paramount Skydance’s Angie Sharma will lead franchise and kids & family, building one plan per franchise across content, consumer products, games, and experiences, with kids & family viewed as a key growth priority.

Jamal Salmon, who comes from Paramount Skydance, will lead data & analytics for marketing, and will also report to Josh Goldstine, president of worldwide marketing & distribution, who will also be responsible for film marketing. Salmon will also have a dotted line to Hugh Williams, chief engineer and evp for consumer and monetization engineering; and Josh Walker, chief financial and strategy officer for direct to consumer. The DTC unit oversees streaming and video-on-demand.

Paramount Skydance’s Michelle Hagen will lead brand partnerships, with Julie Moore, evp of brand partnerships, reporting to her, and Dana Nussbaum, co-head of global motion pictures marketing, Warner Bros., focusing on theatrical. Malene Sam, svp, originals, who was part of Paramount Skydance, report both to Hagen and Shauna Spenley, who leads DTC media. Under Spenley, Sam will be focused on TV and DTC.

Finally, Seth Goren, who comes from WBD, will lead media, bringing together media strategy, media buying, and agency management across the company.  He will report to Mall. But for theatrical media work, he also will report to Nussbaum. For his DTC media work, he will report to Spenley, and for his TV work, he will have a reporting line to Mike Benson, who leads TV Marketing.

The realignment of the marketing team has led to the departure of Meg Maro, the global head of franchise. Mall thanked her for her contributions during her time at the company.

Maro concluded saying: “We have an extraordinary opportunity to redefine what marketing can achieve in our industry, and I can’t wait to see what we accomplish together.”

#7292. Technical Account Manager (APJ) at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re seeking a Technical Account Manager (TAM) to be the primary technical partner for our Team & customers after the sale, helping them adopt, operate, and get long-term value from Supabase.

At Supabase, TAMs are the technical owners of their assigned accounts, working closely with our most valuable customers to:

Understand each customer’s architecture, use cases, constraints, and goals.

Validate deployments and guide customers through onboarding and early success.

Proactively surface risks and optimization opportunities before they become incidents.

Coordinate internal teams on complex escalations and drive root cause analysis.

Align Supabase capabilities with the customer’s roadmap.

Successful TAMs are proactive, technically credible, and customer-first. TAM is a relationship, not a response channel: you’ll act as an extension of your customers’ teams and advocate on their behalf within Supabase.

You’ll be joining our Success team, where you will be expected to lead and innovate around the following areas of responsibility:

Act as the technical owner for your assigned accounts, documenting environments, mapping dependencies, and tracking technical risks.

Validate architecture and deployment plans after the sale, guide initial configuration and best practices, and make sure customers understand platform capabilities and limitations.

Help customers reach early success milestones to shorten time to value and reduce early friction.

Regularly review customer environments and usage patterns to identify scalability, performance, reliability, security, and compliance risks and opportunities.

Plan upgrades and lifecycle events, and run architecture reviews for upcoming initiatives, including capacity and growth planning, feature readiness, and early identification of technical blockers.

Coordinate across Support and Engineering on complex or high-impact issues. TAMs are not frontline support, but provide the context, prioritization, and follow-through that drive resolution, root cause analysis, and preventative action.

Serve as the voice of the customer to Engineering and Product, sharing specific, reproducible feedback on recurring pain points and taking part in beta and early access programs.

Partner with Sales and Customer Success on account health and retention, with technical input on renewals, expansions, and evaluations, and early flags on risks to satisfaction.

6+ years of relevant work experience in engineering, SRE, technical consulting, or similar, including operating production systems under real constraints.

Strong technical depth across database internals, scalability, security posture, and infrastructure tradeoffs. You understand a customer’s architecture as well as they do, without escalating every question.

A proactive mindset: you spot risks and optimization opportunities before the customer does, and you treat your accounts like a personal portfolio.

Sound escalation judgment: you know when to escalate, when to resolve internally, and how to communicate clearly under pressure without creating noise.

Very strong communication skills. TAMs explain complex issues to non-technical executives and root causes to senior engineers, often in the same day.

Awareness of how TAM performance shows up in retention and NRR, including surfacing expansion signals and renewal risks early.

Experience working in async or globally distributed teams.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

He added, “This is a bittersweet goodbye to Joe, Mika, and the place that made possible everything else in my career over the last two decades, but I am thrilled to settle in with my family at Today and dedicate all of my time to that special place.”

Geist was the only remaining on-air talent still pulling double duty for MS NOW and NBC News after NBCUniversal spun off its cable network assets into a new media company, Versant, in 2025.

#7274. The Art, Science and Theater of Modern Marketing ft. Thomas Ranese

What if the best marketing advantage you have is something that cannot be replicated by an algorithm?

For Thomas Ranese, CMO of Intuit, it starts with theater, empathy and understanding what people are actually trying to achieve.

In this episode of Marketing Vanguard, Jenny Rooney sits down with Thomas to unpack how an unconventional career spanning theater, Google and Uber shaped his approach to marketing leadership.

From growing up behind the counter of his father’s pasta shop to leading marketing at one of the world’s biggest financial technology platforms, Thomas gets into why emotional connection can be just as powerful as performance data.

He also addresses why AI needs human expertise to earn customer trust, and why brands should bring more humanity, creativity, and purpose into modern marketing.

Thomas Ranese is Chief Marketing Officer at Intuit, a global technology platform serving millions of customers worldwide with TurboTax, QuickBooks, Credit Karma and Mailchimp.

His career has taken him through some very different worlds, including theater, consulting, government and nonprofit work, Google and Uber. His approach to marketing is rooted in a simple idea: understand what people are trying to achieve, then build brands and experiences that help them get there. He strongly believes in storytelling and using humor to make complex information more relatable.

At Intuit, Thomas is helping shape the company’s evolution into an integrated, AI-powered financial technology platform, with a focus on combining technology with human expertise to help consumers and businesses prosper.

07:18 Marketing Is Theater, and It Goes Beyond Advertising — Thomas breaks down the three different kinds of theater that shape modern marketing: the storytelling and drama of advertising, the emotions and interactions built into customer experience, and the immersive experiences brands create through retail. He also believes real-world human interactions will become increasingly important as more of our lives move into digital and AI-powered environments.

12:44 What a Pasta Shop Taught Him About Customer Obsession — Thomas traces his interest in marketing back to his childhood, when he spent time working in his father’s small pasta shop. He loved being behind the counter, helping customers and figuring out what they needed. He even created his father’s first promotional newsletter on a Commodore 64. Looking back, he sees those early experiences as the foundation of his approach to customer connection, communication and service.

16:01 Why AI Needs Human Intelligence Too — Intuit is building AI into the core of its financial technology platform, but Thomas is particularly focused on the role human experts play alongside it. When customers are making important decisions about taxes, credit or their businesses, getting the right answer matters. AI can provide powerful technology and efficiency, but human expertise brings verification, empathy and the confidence customers need to act.

19:57 Money Is a Means to an End. So Is Your Product — Thomas challenges brands to think beyond what their products do and focus on what customers want to achieve with them. Small business owners aren’t necessarily starting businesses because they want to make money; they’re pursuing a craft, building a lifestyle, serving customers or creating opportunities for their families. The same principle applies across categories: brands become more meaningful when they understand the bigger ambitions their products help people achieve.

22:00 Intuit’s Goal for Financial Literacy — Thomas explains how Intuit is connecting its mission to power prosperity with financial literacy education. Through Intuit for Education, the company aims to reach 50 million students by 2030, addressing the fact that only 32% of students graduate able to pass a basic financial literacy exam. For Thomas, this is an opportunity to create meaningful social impact while building trust and introducing a new generation to the tools that can help them succeed.

26:02 Why CMOs Should be Fully Involved — Thomas pushes back on the idea that senior marketing leaders should step away from execution as their organizations grow. With technology and AI changing marketing so quickly, he believes CMOs need to stay hands-on, understand the tools and continue learning. He argues that the bigger the organization, the more important this becomes, especially when it is easy to become disconnected from the actual work and the customers being served.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

Plus, why fragmentation is getting in the way of better consumer experiences.

From an official day off work from Logitech to the “Spice City” sandwich from Popeyes, brands are nudging their way into the conversation.

#7267. The Media RFP Is Quietly Becoming Obsolete ft. Bruce Williams

Media used to be one part of the marketing machine, but this approach became outdated a while ago.

In this episode of Marketing Vanguard, Jenny Rooney sits down with Bruce Williams, CEO of dentsu X North America, to examine how media is becoming the connective tissue across creative, commerce, CRM, data, and technology.

Bruce reveals why standalone media RFPs are disappearing, and what it really takes to design connected consumer experiences.

He also addresses why brands need to move from channel-first planning toward operating systems built around outcomes.

Bruce Williams is CEO of dentsu X North America. Here, his focus is increasingly on helping brands move beyond isolated media planning toward connected operating models that bring together data, content, technology and consumer experience.

With more than 20 years in media, Bruce has spent much of his career working at the intersection of media strategy, integrated marketing, technology and organizational transformation.

He also works with clients on AI-enabled transformation, agency interoperability and new ways to turn data into faster decision-making at both global and local levels.

01:26 The “Media RFP” Is Quietly Becoming Obsolete — Bruce says media RFPs rarely arrive as standalone asks anymore. Brands increasingly want integrated briefs that connect media with creative, experience, CRM and loyalty, or they want agencies to solve bigger transformation problems. So, the agency conversation has to shift from “Can you buy media well?” to “Can you help us operate better?”

03:13 Your Customer Doesn’t Experience Your Brand in Silos — Marketing may still be organized into separate teams, systems and channels, but the person receiving the experience does not care about those distinctions. Bruce argues that fragmentation remains one of the industry’s longest-running problems because disconnected structures eventually show up as disconnected consumer experiences.

06:22 On AI: Build the System, Then Stay Scrappy — Bruce does not see AI transformation as a choice between massive infrastructure projects and quick tactical wins. He points to work with Heineken that combines long-term data and technology integration with practical tools like local intelligence agents that move competitive and market data directly into local decisions.

08:51 Why Agencies Can’t Keep Saying “Our Way or Nothing” — For Bruce, the future of agency relationships comes down to interoperability and optionality. Closed systems create friction. Agencies need to work directly with brand data, technology and other partners instead of expecting clients to conform to one proprietary model. Openness is becoming part of the value proposition.
